[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came in because my eating has started feeling really out of control, especially when I’m by myself. Like after work or after class, I’ll sit down with my laptop thinking I’m just going to eat dinner and get some stuff done, and then somehow it turns into this whole thing.\n\nI’ll order a full takeout meal, and then I’ll also have chips, and something sweet, and then I’ll start going through whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. It ends up being way, way more than I meant to eat. And it’s not how I’d eat if someone was sitting there with me. I feel embarrassed even saying that, but that’s basically what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is exactly the right word every single time. Sometimes I do, yeah, but it’s more like this heavy, embarrassed feeling afterward, like I don’t really want to think about what I just ate or how much it was.\n\nI’ll kind of sit there and feel gross about it, and then I start replaying it in my head, like, why did I keep going, why didn’t I just stop after dinner, why did I order that much in the first place. But I don’t always have a clear thought of, like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this uncomfortable shame feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on these occasions, or does it feel like you're unable to control how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it definitely feels like I can’t really stop once it gets going. Like, there’s a point where I’m not even hungry anymore, and I know that. I’ll literally be thinking, Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish, you’re not even enjoying this anymore, and at the same time I’m still reaching for the next thing.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I black out or don’t know what I’m doing. I know exactly what I’m doing, which almost makes it feel worse. I’ll tell myself, okay, after this handful of chips, I’m done, or after this dessert, I’m closing everything and getting back to work. But then I just keep going, like my brain has already decided I’m continuing even though another part of me is trying to hit the brakes. So… yeah, I’d say it feels pretty out of my control in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes where you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m honestly not totally sure. I haven’t really timed it or anything, and when it’s happening I’m usually kind of distracted, like I’m on my laptop or watching something, so I don’t know if I’m eating fast in the way you mean.\n\nI do know it can feel like I’m just sort of going from one thing to the next without much of a pause, but I’m not always, like, shoveling food in as quickly as possible. Sometimes it’s more drawn out, like I keep eating over a longer stretch and then suddenly realize how much is gone. So… maybe sometimes? But I wouldn’t say I’ve specifically noticed speed as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t weigh myself consistently enough to say, like, “yes, I gained this many pounds because of these episodes.” I kind of avoid the scale most of the time because I don’t like how I feel when I start focusing on the number too much.\n\nMy clothes… sometimes I’ll notice they feel a little different, like tighter after a stretch where I’ve had more of those nights, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight change or just feeling bloated or uncomfortable or me being in my head about it. So I don’t want to say there’s been some huge obvious change, because I don’t really know.\n\nI guess what I can say is I’ve wondered about it, and I worry about it sometimes, but I haven’t tracked it closely enough to give you a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of the thing that made me finally feel like I should talk to someone about it, because it’s not just having a snack or eating a little more than I meant to.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work or after class, when I’m finally sitting down with my laptop, and I’ll tell myself I’m just going to eat dinner while I get some stuff done. But then it turns into, like, a full takeout meal, and then chips, and then something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer. And it’s not spread out over a whole day or anything—it’s usually in that evening window, like within a couple hours while I’m sitting there.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would plan to eat. It’s also more than I would eat if someone else were there, which is embarrassing to admit. If I were with a friend or at work around other people, I would not eat all of that. But alone, it’s like I keep adding one more thing, and then another, and then I look at what’s gone and I’m like… that was a lot. So yes, I’d say it is a large amount in a short period, at least compared to what feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes even when you're not hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. It’s not even always about being hungry at that point. Like, I can tell I’m full, or at least I’m not hungry anymore, and I’ll still keep reaching for something else.\n\nIt feels weird because in my head I’m still aware of it. I’ll literally be thinking, Jaime, stop. You have that budget report to finish, you’re not even enjoying this anymore, why are you still eating? And then I’ll still open another package or go back for something sweet or salty, whatever’s there. It almost feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of deciding to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll make little bargains with myself too, like, “Okay, this is the last thing,” and then a few minutes later I’m already thinking about the next thing. Or I’ll tell myself I’m going to put it away, and I might even close the bag, but then I reopen it. So yes, I think the control part is a big part of it. Not like I black out or don’t know what I’m doing, but like I can’t seem to get myself to stop even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer “often,” I guess. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like this heavy, embarrassed feeling, like I don’t really want to look at the wrappers or think about what I just ate. Sometimes I’ll feel guilty in the sense of, like, “Why did I do that again?” or “I said I wasn’t going to let it happen tonight.” But other times I’m almost just tired or numb afterward, and then the guilt kind of shows up later when I’m thinking about it.\n\nAnd because it usually happens when I’m by myself, there’s this extra layer of shame around it, like I’m relieved no one saw it but also upset that I was hiding it in the first place. So… yes, I think guilt is part of it, but I don’t know how to measure how often. It’s not like I write it down or anything. It’s more that afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself cause problems in your daily life or affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I mean, I think that’s one of the parts that’s been getting harder to ignore, because it’s not just something that happens and then disappears.\n\nI’ve started avoiding plans with friends or family, especially anything involving food. Like, my friends sometimes do these cooking nights or casual dinners where everyone brings something, and I’ve been making excuses not to go. I’ll say I’m tired from work or I have school stuff to catch up on, which is sometimes true, but not the whole reason. The real reason is I get this anxious feeling that they’re going to somehow notice something is off—like they’ll notice how I’m eating, or wonder why I’m being weird around food, or somehow know how much I’ve been eating when I’m alone.\n\nAnd I know that probably doesn’t make complete sense, because obviously they don’t actually see what happens when I’m by myself. But I feel like I’m carrying it around, if that makes sense. Like I feel exposed even when nobody knows anything. So instead of going and trying to act normal, I just stay home, and then I end up being more isolated outside of work and school.\n\nIt also affects how I feel about myself a lot. I feel embarrassed, and kind of like I’m hiding this whole part of my life. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from people over it, but I also don’t know how to explain why I’m doing that without feeling ashamed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself happen?\n\nPatient: I think… most weeks, honestly. I didn’t want to admit that at first, because if I just think about it vaguely, I can tell myself it’s just “sometimes” or only when I’m stressed. But when I actually looked back, like through my journal and even my food delivery charges, it was harder to pretend it was random.\n\nSince the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks. Usually more than once in a week, too. Not necessarily every single day or anything like that, but it’s not just here and there. There will be a week where it happens maybe a couple of times, and then I’ll tell myself I’m getting it together, and then it happens again the next week.\n\nSeeing the delivery charges was kind of embarrassing, because it made it feel more concrete. Like I couldn’t just say, “Oh, I had a bad night.” It’s been a pattern. So… yeah, most weeks, and usually more than once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s every single episode, no. Sometimes right afterward I’m honestly just sort of blank, or tired, or like I don’t want to think about it at all. The guilt might come later, like when I see the containers or when I’m trying to go to sleep and replaying it.\n\nBut there are also times when I do feel guilty pretty much immediately, especially if I had told myself earlier that day that I wasn’t going to do it. Then it’s like this immediate “I messed up again” feeling.\n\nSo… not every time in the exact same way. I’d say it’s common, but I don’t want to say every episode because sometimes it’s more shame or embarrassment than guilt, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating during these episodes even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. Because it’s not always like I’m hungry in a normal way. Once I start, it’s like something clicks off, or maybe clicks on, I don’t know how to explain it.\n\nI’ll be eating and there’s this part of me that’s fully aware, like, Jaime, stop. You have a budget report to finish. You’re not even enjoying this anymore. And I’ll still keep reaching for the next thing. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it, if that makes sense. Like I’m not completely disconnected, because I know what I’m doing, but knowing doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll even think, okay, this is the last bite, or after this container I’m done, and then I’ll just keep going anyway. And the whole time I’m kind of arguing with myself in my head. It’s not peaceful or satisfying at that point. It feels more like I’m stuck in it until it’s over.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating a lot by yourself been happening at least once a week for the past 3 months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I didn’t want to answer that too quickly because part of me keeps wanting to say, “Well, maybe not every single week,” but when I actually looked back instead of just guessing, it really has been most weeks.\n\nI checked my journal, and then I looked at my food delivery charges, which was honestly kind of humiliating, but it made it harder to explain away. Since the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks, and usually more than once in the same week. So I don’t want to say there’s been some huge obvious change, because I don’t really know.\n\nI guess what I can say is I’ve wondered about it, and I worry about it sometimes, but I haven’t tracked it closely enough to give you a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of the thing that made me finally feel like I should talk to someone about it, because it’s not just having a snack or eating a little more than I meant to.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work or after class, when I’m finally sitting down with my laptop, and I’ll tell myself I’m just going to eat dinner while I get some stuff done. But then it turns into, like, a full takeout meal, and then chips, and then something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer. And it’s not spread out over a whole day or anything—it’s usually in that evening window, like within a couple hours while I’m sitting there.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would plan to eat. It’s also more than I would eat if someone else were there, which is embarrassing to admit. If I were with a friend or at work around other people, I would not eat all of that. But alone, it’s like I keep adding one more thing, and then another, and then I look at what’s gone and I’m like… that was a lot. So yes, I’d say it is a large amount in a short period, at least compared to what feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes even when you're not hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. It’s not even always about being hungry at that point. Like, I can tell I’m full, or at least I’m not hungry anymore, and I’ll still keep reaching for something else.\n\nIt feels weird because in my head I’m still aware of it. I’ll literally be thinking, Jaime, stop. You have that budget report to finish, you’re not even enjoying this anymore, why are you still eating? And then I’ll still open another package or go back for something sweet or salty, whatever’s there. It almost feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of deciding to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll make little bargains with myself too, like, “Okay, this is the last thing,” and then a few minutes later I’m already thinking about the next thing. Or I’ll tell myself I’m going to put it away, and I might even close the bag, but then I reopen it. So yes, I think the control part is a big part of it. Not like I black out or don’t know what I’m doing, but like I can’t seem to get myself to stop even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer “often,” I guess. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like this heavy, embarrassed feeling, like I don’t really want to look at the wrappers or think about what I just ate. Sometimes I’ll feel guilty in the sense of, like, “Why did I do that again?” or “I said I wasn’t going to let it happen tonight.” But other times I’m almost just tired or numb afterward, and then the guilt kind of shows up later when I’m thinking about it.\n\nAnd because it usually happens when I’m by myself, there’s this extra layer of shame around it, like I’m relieved no one saw it but also upset that I was hiding it in the first place. So… yes, I think guilt is part of it, but I don’t know how to measure how often. It’s not like I write it down or anything. It’s more that afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself cause problems in your daily life or affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I mean, I think that’s one of the parts that’s been getting harder to ignore, because it’s not just something that happens and then disappears.\n\nI’ve started avoiding plans with friends or family, especially anything involving food. Like, my friends sometimes do these cooking nights or casual dinners where everyone brings something, and I’ve been making excuses not to go. I’ll say I’m tired from work or I have school stuff to catch up on, which is sometimes true, but not the whole reason. The real reason is I get this anxious feeling that they’re going to somehow notice something is off—like they’ll notice how I’m eating, or wonder why I’m being weird around food, or somehow know how much I’ve been eating when I’m alone.\n\nAnd I know that probably doesn’t make complete sense, because obviously they don’t actually see what happens when I’m by myself. But I feel like I’m carrying it around, if that makes sense. Like I feel exposed even when nobody knows anything. So instead of going and trying to act normal, I just stay home, and then I end up being more isolated outside of work and school.\n\nIt also affects how I feel about myself a lot. I feel embarrassed, and kind of like I’m hiding this whole part of my life. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from people over it, but I also don’t know how to explain why I’m doing that without feeling ashamed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself happen?\n\nPatient: I think… most weeks, honestly. I didn’t want to admit that at first, because if I just think about it vaguely, I can tell myself it’s just “sometimes” or only when I’m stressed. But when I actually looked back, like through my journal and even my food delivery charges, it was harder to pretend it was random.\n\nSince the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks. Usually more than once in a week, too. Not necessarily every single day or anything like that, but it’s not just here and there. There will be a week where it happens maybe a couple of times, and then I’ll tell myself I’m getting it together, and then it happens again the next week.\n\nSeeing the delivery charges was kind of embarrassing, because it made it feel more concrete. Like I couldn’t just say, “Oh, I had a bad night.” It’s been a pattern. So… yeah, most weeks, and usually more than once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s every single episode, no. Sometimes right afterward I’m honestly just sort of blank, or tired, or like I don’t want to think about it at all. The guilt might come later, like when I see the containers or when I’m trying to go to sleep and replaying it.\n\nBut there are also times when I do feel guilty pretty much immediately, especially if I had told myself earlier that day that I wasn’t going to do it. Then it’s like this immediate “I messed up again” feeling.\n\nSo… not every time in the exact same way. I’d say it’s common, but I don’t want to say every episode because sometimes it’s more shame or embarrassment than guilt, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating during these episodes even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. Because it’s not always like I’m hungry in a normal way. Once I start, it’s like something clicks off, or maybe clicks on, I don’t know how to explain it.\n\nI’ll be eating and there’s this part of me that’s fully aware, like, Jaime, stop. You have a budget report to finish. You’re not even enjoying this anymore. And I’ll still keep reaching for the next thing. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it, if that makes sense. Like I’m not completely disconnected, because I know what I’m doing, but knowing doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll even think, okay, this is the last bite, or after this container I’m done, and then I’ll just keep going anyway. And the whole time I’m kind of arguing with myself in my head. It’s not peaceful or satisfying at that point. It feels more like I’m stuck in it until it’s over.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating a lot by yourself been happening at least once a week for the past 3 months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I didn’t want to answer that too quickly because part of me keeps wanting to say, “Well, maybe not every single week,” but when I actually looked back instead of just guessing, it really has been most weeks.\n\nI checked my journal, and then I looked at my food delivery charges, which was honestly kind of humiliating, but it made it harder to explain away. Since the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks, and usually more than once in the same week. It’s not like every day, but it’s also definitely not just an occasional thing here and there.\n\nSo… yes, at least once a week, for that stretch of time. Usually more than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to compensate for these episodes of eating by doing things like exercising, vomiting, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t really think of myself as doing something organized afterward, like a plan to “undo” it. I don’t make myself throw up or anything like that. And I’m not using laxatives for that, no.\n\nWith exercise, it’s a little harder for me to explain because sometimes I do get this panicky feeling afterward, like I need to go walk or move around because I feel so uncomfortable in my body. But I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to cancel it out, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I feel restless or upset and want to get out of my apartment or stop sitting there with the evidence of it, I guess.\n\nSo… no, not vomiting or laxatives. And exercise isn’t really a regular compensation thing, at least not in the way I think you’re asking.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts I feel the most ashamed saying out loud.

I’ve gotten pretty strategic about it, which makes me feel awful, because it means I know what I’m doing. I’ll wait until my roommate is out, or if I’m at home, until my family has gone to bed. It’s like I need the apartment or the kitchen to be empty before I can let myself do it. If someone else is around, I can act totally normal—like I’m just having tea or a reasonable snack or whatever—but I’m also kind of watching the clock, waiting for them to leave or go to their room.

And I think it’s because during the day I try so hard to seem put-together. I’m the person who talks about meal prepping, or says I’m trying to be healthy, or acts like I have everything under control. So the idea of someone seeing how much I can actually eat in one sitting feels… humiliating. Like it would ruin the version of me they think they know.

I do hide the evidence sometimes too, not in some dramatic way, but I’ll make sure wrappers are buried under other trash or I’ll take the trash out so no one sees the containers. I hate even admitting that. It feels so sneaky and childish, but in the moment I just really don’t want anyone to know.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came in because my eating has started feeling really out of control, especially when I’m by myself. Like after work or after class, I’ll sit down with my laptop thinking I’m just going to eat dinner and get some stuff done, and then somehow it turns into this whole thing.\n\nI’ll order a full takeout meal, and then I’ll also have chips, and something sweet, and then I’ll start going through whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. But I don’t always have a clear thought of, like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this uncomfortable shame feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on these occasions, or does it feel like you're unable to control how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it definitely feels like I can’t really stop once it gets going. Like, there’s a point where I’m not even hungry anymore, and I know that. I know exactly what I’m doing, which almost makes it feel worse. I’ll tell myself, okay, after this handful of chips, I’m done, or after this dessert, I’m closing everything and getting back to work. But then I just keep going, like my brain has already decided I’m continuing even though another part of me is trying to hit the brakes. So… yeah, I’d say it feels pretty out of my control in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes where you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m honestly not totally sure. I haven’t really timed it or anything, and when it’s happening I’m usually kind of distracted, like I’m on my laptop or watching something, so I don’t know if I’m eating fast in the way you mean.\n\nI do know it can feel like I’m just sort of going from one thing to the next without much of a pause, but I’m not always, like, shoveling food in as quickly as possible. Sometimes it’s more drawn out, like I keep eating over a longer stretch and then suddenly realize how much is gone. So… maybe sometimes? But I wouldn’t say I’ve specifically noticed speed as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t weigh myself consistently enough to say, like, “yes, I gained this many pounds because of these episodes.” I kind of avoid the scale most of the time because I don’t like how I feel when I start focusing on the number too much.\n\nMy clothes… sometimes I’ll notice they feel a little different, like tighter after a stretch where I’ve had more of those nights, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight change or just feeling bloated or uncomfortable or me being in my head about it. So I don’t want to say there’s been some huge obvious change, because I don’t really know.\n\nI guess what I can say is I’ve wondered about it, and I worry about it sometimes, but I haven’t tracked it closely enough to give you a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of the thing that made me finally feel like I should talk to someone about it, because it’s not just having a snack or eating a little more than I meant to.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work or after class, when I’m finally sitting down with my laptop, and I’ll tell myself I’m just going to eat dinner while I get some stuff done. But then it turns into, like, a full takeout meal, and then chips, and then something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer. And it’s not spread out over a whole day or anything—it’s usually in that evening window, like within a couple hours while I’m sitting there.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would plan to eat. It’s also more than I would eat if someone else were there, which is embarrassing to admit. If I were with a friend or at work around other people, I would not eat all of that. But alone, it’s like I keep adding one more thing, and then another, and then I look at what’s gone and I’m like… that was a lot. So yes, I’d say it is a large amount in a short period, at least compared to what feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes even when you're not hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. It’s not even always about being hungry at that point. Like, I can tell I’m full, or at least I’m not hungry anymore, and I’ll still keep reaching for something else.\n\nIt feels weird because in my head I’m still aware of it. I’ll literally be thinking, Jaime, stop. You have that budget report to finish, you’re not even enjoying this anymore, why are you still eating? And then I’ll still open another package or go back for something sweet or salty, whatever’s there. It almost feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of deciding to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll make little bargains with myself too, like, “Okay, this is the last thing,” and then a few minutes later I’m already thinking about the next thing. Or I’ll tell myself I’m going to put it away, and I might even close the bag, but then I reopen it. So yes, I think the control part is a big part of it. Not like I black out or don’t know what I’m doing, but like I can’t seem to get myself to stop even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer “often,” I guess. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like this heavy, embarrassed feeling, like I don’t really want to look at the wrappers or think about what I just ate. Sometimes I’ll feel guilty in the sense of, like, “Why did I do that again?” or “I said I wasn’t going to let it happen tonight.” But other times I’m almost just tired or numb afterward, and then the guilt kind of shows up later when I’m thinking about it.\n\nAnd because it usually happens when I’m by myself, there’s this extra layer of shame around it, like I’m relieved no one saw it but also upset that I was hiding it in the first place. So… yes, I think guilt is part of it, but I don’t know how to measure how often. It’s not like I write it down or anything. It’s more that afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself cause problems in your daily life or affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I mean, I think that’s one of the parts that’s been getting harder to ignore, because it’s not just something that happens and then disappears.\n\nI’ve started avoiding plans with friends or family, especially anything involving food. Like, my friends sometimes do these cooking nights or casual dinners where everyone brings something, and I’ve been making excuses not to go. I’ll say I’m tired from work or I have school stuff to catch up on, which is sometimes true, but not the whole reason. The real reason is I get this anxious feeling that they’re going to somehow notice something is off—like they’ll notice how I’m eating, or wonder why I’m being weird around food, or somehow know how much I’ve been eating when I’m alone.\n\nAnd I know that probably doesn’t make complete sense, because obviously they don’t actually see what happens when I’m by myself. But I feel like I’m carrying it around, if that makes sense. Like I feel exposed even when nobody knows anything. So instead of going and trying to act normal, I just stay home, and then I end up being more isolated outside of work and school.\n\nIt also affects how I feel about myself a lot. I feel embarrassed, and kind of like I’m hiding this whole part of my life. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from people over it, but I also don’t know how to explain why I’m doing that without feeling ashamed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself happen?\n\nPatient: I think… most weeks, honestly. I didn’t want to admit that at first, because if I just think about it vaguely, I can tell myself it’s just “sometimes” or only when I’m stressed. But when I actually looked back, like through my journal and even my food delivery charges, it was harder to pretend it was random.\n\nSince the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks. Usually more than once in a week, too. Not necessarily every single day or anything like that, but it’s not just here and there. There will be a week where it happens maybe a couple of times, and then I’ll tell myself I’m getting it together, and then it happens again the next week.\n\nSeeing the delivery charges was kind of embarrassing, because it made it feel more concrete. Like I couldn’t just say, “Oh, I had a bad night.” It’s been a pattern. So… yeah, most weeks, and usually more than once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s every single episode, no. Sometimes right afterward I’m honestly just sort of blank, or tired, or like I don’t want to think about it at all. The guilt might come later, like when I see the containers or when I’m trying to go to sleep and replaying it.\n\nBut there are also times when I do feel guilty pretty much immediately, especially if I had told myself earlier that day that I wasn’t going to do it. Then it’s like this immediate “I messed up again” feeling.\n\nSo… not every time in the exact same way. I’d say it’s common, but I don’t want to say every episode because sometimes it’s more shame or embarrassment than guilt, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating during these episodes even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. Because it’s not always like I’m hungry in a normal way. Once I start, it’s like something clicks off, or maybe clicks on, I don’t know how to explain it.\n\nI’ll be eating and there’s this part of me that’s fully aware, like, Jaime, stop. You have a budget report to finish. You’re not even enjoying this anymore. And I’ll still keep reaching for the next thing. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it, if that makes sense. Like I’m not completely disconnected, because I know what I’m doing, but knowing doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll even think, okay, this is the last bite, or after this container I’m done, and then I’ll just keep going anyway. And the whole time I’m kind of arguing with myself in my head. It’s not peaceful or satisfying at that point. It feels more like I’m stuck in it until it’s over.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating a lot by yourself been happening at least once a week for the past 3 months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I didn’t want to answer that too quickly because part of me keeps wanting to say, “Well, maybe not every single week,” but when I actually looked back instead of just guessing, it really has been most weeks.\n\nI checked my journal, and then I looked at my food delivery charges, which was honestly kind of humiliating, but it made it harder to explain away. Since the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks, and usually more than once in the same week. It’s not like every day, but it’s also definitely not just an occasional thing here and there.\n\nSo… yes, at least once a week, for that stretch of time. Usually more than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to compensate for these episodes of eating by doing things like exercising, vomiting, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t really think of myself as doing something organized afterward, like a plan to “undo” it. I don’t make myself throw up or anything like that. And I’m not using laxatives for that, no.\n\nWith exercise, it’s a little harder for me to explain because sometimes I do get this panicky feeling afterward, like I need to go walk or move around because I feel so uncomfortable in my body. But I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to cancel it out, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I feel restless or upset and want to get out of my apartment or stop sitting there with the evidence of it, I guess.\n\nSo… no, not vomiting or laxatives. And exercise isn’t really a regular compensation thing, at least not in the way I think you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret or hiding evidence of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts I feel the most ashamed saying out loud.\n\nI’ve gotten pretty strategic about it, which makes me feel awful, because it means I know what I’m doing. I’ll wait until my roommate is out, or if I’m at home, until my family has gone to bed. It’s like I need the apartment or the kitchen to be empty before I can let myself do it. If someone else is around, I can act totally normal—like I’m just having tea or a reasonable snack or whatever—but I’m also kind of watching the clock, waiting for them to leave or go to their room.\n\nAnd I think it’s because during the day I try so hard to seem put-together. I’m the person who talks about meal prepping, or says I’m trying to be healthy, or acts like I have everything under control. So the idea of someone seeing how much I can actually eat in one sitting feels… humiliating. Like it would ruin the version of me they think they know.\n\nI do hide the evidence sometimes too, not in some dramatic way, but I’ll make sure wrappers are buried under other trash or I’ll take the trash out so no one sees the containers. I hate even admitting that. It feels so sneaky and childish, but in the moment I just really don’t want anyone to know.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

I’ll wait until my roommate is out, or if I’m at home, until my family has gone to bed. It’s like I need the apartment or the kitchen to be empty before I can let myself do it. If someone else is around, I can act totally normal—like I’m just having tea or a reasonable snack or whatever—but I’m also kind of watching the clock, waiting for them to leave or go to their room.\n\nAnd I think it’s because during the day I try so hard to seem put-together. I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I know it feels fast in the sense that it feels kind of rushed and automatic, like I’m not sitting there calmly enjoying food the way I would if I were eating dinner with someone.

    "content": "Candidate Diseases: \n[\"F50.8\"]\n\nInterview Transcript: \nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… I guess I came in because my eating has started feeling really out of control, especially when I’m by myself. Like after work or after class, I’ll sit down with my laptop thinking I’m just going to eat dinner and get some stuff done, and then somehow it turns into this whole thing.\n\nI’ll order a full takeout meal, and then I’ll also have chips, and something sweet, and then I’ll start going through whatever snacks I have in my desk drawer. It ends up being way, way more than I meant to eat. And it’s not how I’d eat if someone was sitting there with me. I feel embarrassed even saying that, but that’s basically what made me think I should talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if “guilty” is exactly the right word every single time. Sometimes I do, yeah, but it’s more like this heavy, embarrassed feeling afterward, like I don’t really want to think about what I just ate or how much it was.\n\nI’ll kind of sit there and feel gross about it, and then I start replaying it in my head, like, why did I keep going, why didn’t I just stop after dinner, why did I order that much in the first place. But I don’t always have a clear thought of, like, “I’m guilty.” It’s more this uncomfortable shame feeling, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on these occasions, or does it feel like you're unable to control how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it definitely feels like I can’t really stop once it gets going. Like, there’s a point where I’m not even hungry anymore, and I know that. I’ll literally be thinking, Jaime, stop, you have that budget report to finish, you’re not even enjoying this anymore, and at the same time I’m still reaching for the next thing.\n\nIt’s weird because it’s not like I black out or don’t know what I’m doing. I know exactly what I’m doing, which almost makes it feel worse. I’ll tell myself, okay, after this handful of chips, I’m done, or after this dessert, I’m closing everything and getting back to work. But then I just keep going, like my brain has already decided I’m continuing even though another part of me is trying to hit the brakes. So… yeah, I’d say it feels pretty out of my control in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really fast during these episodes where you can't seem to stop?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m honestly not totally sure. I haven’t really timed it or anything, and when it’s happening I’m usually kind of distracted, like I’m on my laptop or watching something, so I don’t know if I’m eating fast in the way you mean.\n\nI do know it can feel like I’m just sort of going from one thing to the next without much of a pause, but I’m not always, like, shoveling food in as quickly as possible. Sometimes it’s more drawn out, like I keep eating over a longer stretch and then suddenly realize how much is gone. So… maybe sometimes? But I wouldn’t say I’ve specifically noticed speed as the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I mean, I don’t weigh myself consistently enough to say, like, “yes, I gained this many pounds because of these episodes.” I kind of avoid the scale most of the time because I don’t like how I feel when I start focusing on the number too much.\n\nMy clothes… sometimes I’ll notice they feel a little different, like tighter after a stretch where I’ve had more of those nights, but then I don’t know if that’s actual weight change or just feeling bloated or uncomfortable or me being in my head about it. So I don’t want to say there’s been some huge obvious change, because I don’t really know.\n\nI guess what I can say is I’ve wondered about it, and I worry about it sometimes, but I haven’t tracked it closely enough to give you a clear answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within a couple of hours?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, that’s kind of the thing that made me finally feel like I should talk to someone about it, because it’s not just having a snack or eating a little more than I meant to.\n\nA lot of times it’s after work or after class, when I’m finally sitting down with my laptop, and I’ll tell myself I’m just going to eat dinner while I get some stuff done. But then it turns into, like, a full takeout meal, and then chips, and then something sweet, and then whatever random snacks I have in my desk drawer. And it’s not spread out over a whole day or anything—it’s usually in that evening window, like within a couple hours while I’m sitting there.\n\nAnd it’s definitely more than I would plan to eat. It’s also more than I would eat if someone else were there, which is embarrassing to admit. If I were with a friend or at work around other people, I would not eat all of that. But alone, it’s like I keep adding one more thing, and then another, and then I look at what’s gone and I’m like… that was a lot. So yes, I’d say it is a large amount in a short period, at least compared to what feels normal for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what or how much you're eating during these episodes even when you're not hungry anymore?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that part I do notice. It’s not even always about being hungry at that point. Like, I can tell I’m full, or at least I’m not hungry anymore, and I’ll still keep reaching for something else.\n\nIt feels weird because in my head I’m still aware of it. I’ll literally be thinking, Jaime, stop. You have that budget report to finish, you’re not even enjoying this anymore, why are you still eating? And then I’ll still open another package or go back for something sweet or salty, whatever’s there. It almost feels like I’m watching myself do it instead of deciding to do it, if that makes sense.\n\nI’ll make little bargains with myself too, like, “Okay, this is the last thing,” and then a few minutes later I’m already thinking about the next thing. Or I’ll tell myself I’m going to put it away, and I might even close the bag, but then I reopen it. So yes, I think the control part is a big part of it. Not like I black out or don’t know what I’m doing, but like I can’t seem to get myself to stop even when I really want to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty after these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer “often,” I guess. I do feel bad afterward sometimes, but I don’t know if I’d call it guilt every single time, if that makes sense.\n\nA lot of the time it’s more like this heavy, embarrassed feeling, like I don’t really want to look at the wrappers or think about what I just ate. Sometimes I’ll feel guilty in the sense of, like, “Why did I do that again?” or “I said I wasn’t going to let it happen tonight.” But other times I’m almost just tired or numb afterward, and then the guilt kind of shows up later when I’m thinking about it.\n\nAnd because it usually happens when I’m by myself, there’s this extra layer of shame around it, like I’m relieved no one saw it but also upset that I was hiding it in the first place. So… yes, I think guilt is part of it, but I don’t know how to measure how often. It’s not like I write it down or anything. It’s more that afterward I usually don’t feel good about myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself cause problems in your daily life or affect how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it does. I mean, I think that’s one of the parts that’s been getting harder to ignore, because it’s not just something that happens and then disappears.\n\nI’ve started avoiding plans with friends or family, especially anything involving food. Like, my friends sometimes do these cooking nights or casual dinners where everyone brings something, and I’ve been making excuses not to go. I’ll say I’m tired from work or I have school stuff to catch up on, which is sometimes true, but not the whole reason. The real reason is I get this anxious feeling that they’re going to somehow notice something is off—like they’ll notice how I’m eating, or wonder why I’m being weird around food, or somehow know how much I’ve been eating when I’m alone.\n\nAnd I know that probably doesn’t make complete sense, because obviously they don’t actually see what happens when I’m by myself. But I feel like I’m carrying it around, if that makes sense. Like I feel exposed even when nobody knows anything. So instead of going and trying to act normal, I just stay home, and then I end up being more isolated outside of work and school.\n\nIt also affects how I feel about myself a lot. I feel embarrassed, and kind of like I’m hiding this whole part of my life. I don’t like that I’m pulling away from people over it, but I also don’t know how to explain why I’m doing that without feeling ashamed.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating a lot by yourself happen?\n\nPatient: I think… most weeks, honestly. I didn’t want to admit that at first, because if I just think about it vaguely, I can tell myself it’s just “sometimes” or only when I’m stressed. But when I actually looked back, like through my journal and even my food delivery charges, it was harder to pretend it was random.\n\nSince the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks. Usually more than once in a week, too. Not necessarily every single day or anything like that, but it’s not just here and there. There will be a week where it happens maybe a couple of times, and then I’ll tell myself I’m getting it together, and then it happens again the next week.\n\nSeeing the delivery charges was kind of embarrassing, because it made it feel more concrete. Like I couldn’t just say, “Oh, I had a bad night.” It’s been a pattern. So… yeah, most weeks, and usually more than once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after every episode of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s every single episode, no. Sometimes right afterward I’m honestly just sort of blank, or tired, or like I don’t want to think about it at all. The guilt might come later, like when I see the containers or when I’m trying to go to sleep and replaying it.\n\nBut there are also times when I do feel guilty pretty much immediately, especially if I had told myself earlier that day that I wasn’t going to do it. Then it’s like this immediate “I messed up again” feeling.\n\nSo… not every time in the exact same way. I’d say it’s common, but I don’t want to say every episode because sometimes it’s more shame or embarrassment than guilt, if that distinction makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating during these episodes even if you want to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s probably the part that scares me the most, honestly. Because it’s not always like I’m hungry in a normal way. Once I start, it’s like something clicks off, or maybe clicks on, I don’t know how to explain it.\n\nI’ll be eating and there’s this part of me that’s fully aware, like, Jaime, stop. You have a budget report to finish. You’re not even enjoying this anymore. And I’ll still keep reaching for the next thing. It feels almost like I’m watching myself do it, if that makes sense. Like I’m not completely disconnected, because I know what I’m doing, but knowing doesn’t seem to make me able to stop.\n\nSometimes I’ll even think, okay, this is the last bite, or after this container I’m done, and then I’ll just keep going anyway. And the whole time I’m kind of arguing with myself in my head. It’s not peaceful or satisfying at that point. It feels more like I’m stuck in it until it’s over.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating a lot by yourself been happening at least once a week for the past 3 months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I mean, I didn’t want to answer that too quickly because part of me keeps wanting to say, “Well, maybe not every single week,” but when I actually looked back instead of just guessing, it really has been most weeks.\n\nI checked my journal, and then I looked at my food delivery charges, which was honestly kind of humiliating, but it made it harder to explain away. Since the restructuring started at work, it’s been happening most weeks, and usually more than once in the same week. It’s not like every day, but it’s also definitely not just an occasional thing here and there.\n\nSo… yes, at least once a week, for that stretch of time. Usually more than that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever try to compensate for these episodes of eating by doing things like exercising, vomiting, or using laxatives?\n\nPatient: I’m… not totally sure how to answer that, because I don’t really think of myself as doing something organized afterward, like a plan to “undo” it. I don’t make myself throw up or anything like that. And I’m not using laxatives for that, no.\n\nWith exercise, it’s a little harder for me to explain because sometimes I do get this panicky feeling afterward, like I need to go walk or move around because I feel so uncomfortable in my body. But I wouldn’t say I’m doing intense workouts to cancel it out, if that’s what you mean. It’s more like I feel restless or upset and want to get out of my apartment or stop sitting there with the evidence of it, I guess.\n\nSo… no, not vomiting or laxatives. And exercise isn’t really a regular compensation thing, at least not in the way I think you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in secret or hiding evidence of your eating during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do. That’s actually one of the parts I feel the most ashamed saying out loud.\n\nI’ve gotten pretty strategic about it, which makes me feel awful, because it means I know what I’m doing. I’ll wait until my roommate is out, or if I’m at home, until my family has gone to bed. It’s like I need the apartment or the kitchen to be empty before I can let myself do it. If someone else is around, I can act totally normal—like I’m just having tea or a reasonable snack or whatever—but I’m also kind of watching the clock, waiting for them to leave or go to their room.\n\nAnd I think it’s because during the day I try so hard to seem put-together. I’m the person who talks about meal prepping, or says I’m trying to be healthy, or acts like I have everything under control. So the idea of someone seeing how much I can actually eat in one sitting feels… humiliating. Like it would ruin the version of me they think they know.\n\nI do hide the evidence sometimes too, not in some dramatic way, but I’ll make sure wrappers are buried under other trash or I’ll take the trash out so no one sees the containers. I hate even admitting that. It feels so sneaky and childish, but in the moment I just really don’t want anyone to know.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically eat rapidly during these episodes of eating a lot by yourself?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I mean… I know it feels fast in the sense that it feels kind of rushed and automatic, like I’m not sitting there calmly enjoying food the way I would if I were eating dinner with someone.\n\nBut I haven’t really timed it or paid close attention to how fast I’m chewing or anything like that. Sometimes afterward I’ll realize a lot of food is gone and it didn’t feel like that much time passed, but I don’t know if that means I’m eating rapidly in the way you mean clinically.\n\nI think in the moment I’m not very aware of the pace. I’m more focused on just… continuing, if that makes sense. So I guess my answer is maybe, or probably sometimes, but I don’t feel confident saying it definitely happens every time."
